[{"question_id": "8B.10", "answer": "By the end of the supplied period, substantial parts of the program had become budgets, transfer systems, operating rules, labor and pension measures, military and National Guard roles, administrative structures, and infrastructure commitments. This did not establish broad durable achievement: health and education capacity weakened, security and energy objectives remained unresolved, projects had cost, environmental, legal, or operational weaknesses, measurement and oversight were deficient, and some institutional changes were invalidated or dependent on personal or military control. Officials apologized for the delayed and said it was due to trains being “reconfigured.”\nSome passengers napped on the floor of the concrete platform. Some — many self-declared supporters of President Andrés Manuel López Obrador — cheered when the train finally appeared in Cancun.\nThe train running in the other direction from the Gulf coast colonial city of Campeche was also delayed for hours, because only one side of the planned double rail line is finished. Officials estimated it would take about 5 1/2 hours to travel the 290 miles (473 kilometers) from Campeche to Cancun.\nRelated Coverage\nMexico’s president inaugurates first part of $20 billion tourist train project on Yucatan peninsula\nWorries abound that Mexico’s Maya Train will destroy jungle\nMexican officials admit secrecy-shrouded border train project had no environmental impact study\nMeanwhile, the cost of the project has soared from original estimates of around $8.5 billion, to as much as $28 billion.\nThe 950-mile line,\ncalled the Maya Train\n, is meant to connect beach resorts and archaeological sites. However, only about one-third of it — the 290-mile (473-kilometer) stretch that President Andrés Manuel López Obrador inaugurated with dignitaries and the press Friday — has even been partly finished.\nOfficials pledged the rest of the line would be ready by the end of February.\nBut even on the part López Obrador inaugurated Friday\n, just a single line of a planned double-line track has been finished, meaning one train has to wait on a siding while another passes.\nThe stretch running between Campeche and Cancun is about one-third of the entire project and covers the least controversial portion of the route, which crosses many environmentally sensitive areas.\nRead More\nA first-class ticket on one of the two trains from Cancun to Merida, the most popular stop, will cost the equivalent of $68. A first-class bus covers the route in about the same time and costs about $58, with buses leaving about every half hour from the city centers, rather than remote train stations on the outskirts.\nThat led to questions about whether the train will ever cover its operating costs, much less its construction budget.\nMexico’s army, which operates the train and built part of the railway, did not respond to a request for comment about the delays or cost overruns.\n\nSign up for Morning Wire:\nOur flagship newsletter breaks down the biggest headlines of the day.\nEmail address\nSign up\nBy checking this box, you agree to AP's\nTerms of Use\nand acknowledge that AP may collect and use your data pursuant to our\nPrivacy Policy\n.\nIn comments Friday, López Obrador acknowledged, “Yes, things are lacking (on the train), of course,” and predicted it would take “three years, four years” for the train system to begin covering its operating costs under the best scenario.\nAsked how much the construction cost of the project would be, the president said: “I don’t know ... I don’t have the exact figure.”\nOriginally projected to cost $8.6 billion, by now $22.7 billion has been assigned to the project, and Treasury Secretary Rogelio Ramírez told local media the final cost would be around $28 billion.\nUnlike the remaining two-thirds of the Maya Train, the part of the line inaugurated Friday already had an old train line running over much of the route. Many of the still-unfinished parts were cut through the jungle and built over sensitive, relic-filled cave systems, drawing objections from environmentalists.\nLópez Obrador has raced to finish the Maya Train project before he leaves office in September, rolling over the objections of ecologists, cave divers and archaeologists.\nThe train runs along the Caribbean coast and\nthreatens extensive caves\nwhere some of the oldest human remains in North America have been discovered. Because of the region’s limestone geology, it is riddled with caves that carry most of its water.\nWhile officials have touted the train as utilitarian transport for freight and local residents, its only real source of significant income would be tourists. However, given its frequent stops, unwieldy route and lack of feasibility studies, it is unclear how many tourists will actually want to buy tickets.\n“The train won’t help residents get to work or school, and besides, it’s very expensive,” SELVAME, a coalition of groups opposing the project, said in a statement Friday. “The train runs through the jungle, filling cenotes (sinkhole lakes) and underground rivers with concrete, without any studies.”\nLópez Obrador has tried to rush through the\nproject\nby exempting it from normal permitting, public reporting and environmental impact statements, claiming it is vital to national security.\nIn November 2021, his administration issued a broad decree requiring all federal agencies to give automatic approval for any public works project the government deems to be “in the national interest” or to “involve national security.”\n____\nFollow AP’s coverage of Latin America and the Caribbean at", "excerpt_sha256": "58b18879db9bd40b145e50ac691064d7311d1c3701194ab08729127ea790e500", "verification_status": "accepted", "verification_notes": []}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0049-E006", "source_id": "SRC-0fdf5cfbde266d7b", "url": "https://www.coneval.org.mx/EvaluacionDS/MejorasUso/IPP/Documents/ANALISIS_PEF_2022_2023_PR.pdf", "title": "Análisis del PEF 2022-2023: programas de desarrollo social vinculados con pobreza y derechos", "publisher": "CONEVAL", "raw_sha256": "6dc7f8b0e752797426c89cb98ed40b36f6e026ae09e2f57c5a70c3d3f7b7582d", "fact_summary": "CONEVAL reported that the 2023 federal budget contained 121 social programs and increased their nominal budget by 16.2% over 2022. de las acciones del Gobierno Federal en un ejercicio fiscal.\n   • El Ejecutivo Federal a través de la Secretaría de Hacienda y Crédito Público (SHCP)\n     entregó el pasado 8 de septiembre el                         S281 Programa Nacional de Reconstrucción\n\n\nFuente: elaboración del CONEVAL con información del PEF 2022 y 2023.\nNota: el presupuesto de los programas se presenta en Millones de Pesos (MDP).\n\nProgramas prioritarios de desarrollo social\nAnálisis del PEF 2022-2023\n •   Al inicio de la Administración Pública Federal vigente, se establecieron 17 programas prioritarios para\n     el Gobierno Federal, de los cuales 15 continúan en el PEF 2023.\n      o En 2022, el Programa de Microcréditos para el Bienestar (S285) de BIENESTAR no contó con\n        presupuesto aprobado, sin embargo operó en 2022. De acuerdo al PEF 2023 este programa ya no\n        aparece.\n •   El presupuesto total de los programas prioritarios incrementó 28.1 por ciento en términos nominales, al\n     pasar de $506,032.4 MDP en 2022 a $648,136.8 MDP en el ejercicio fiscal 2023.\n      o 14 programas prioritarios tienen un incremento en su presupuesto, siendo el programa de\n        Fertilizantes (S292) de la SADER el que presenta mayor aumento.\n      o El programa prioritario con más presupuesto es Pensión para el Bienestar de las Personas\n        Adultas Mayores (S176) de BIENESTAR.\n •   Del total de los programas prioritarios, solo uno redujo su presupuesto, este es el Programa Nacional\n     de Reconstrucción (S281) de la Secretaría de Desarrollo Agrario, Territorial y Urbano (SEDATU). En\n     este programa solo se incluye los ramos 12 (Salud), 15 (SEDATU) y 48 (Cultura), excluyendo al ramo\n     11 (SEP).\n      •   Este programa tuvo una disminución en su presupuesto de 36.22 por ciento respecto al PEF\n          2022.\n      •   Adicionalmente, este es el programa prioritario de menor presupuesto en el PEF 2023.\n\nProgramas de desarrollo social vinculados con pobreza y derechos\n  Análisis del PEF 2022-2023\n           De los 120 programas que operaron en 2022 y a partir de la metodología de contribución de los programas y acciones de desarrollo social\n           en la disminución de la pobreza y en el acceso efectivo a los derechos sociales5, el CONEVAL determinó lo siguiente:\n           •       Los 536 programas vinculados en la reducción de la pobreza en 2022 continúan en 2023, incrementando su presupuesto 18.15 por\n                   ciento.\n           •       En 2022 se identificaron 976 programas vinculados en el acceso efectivo a los derechos sociales, los cuales continúan en 2023\n                   aumentando su presupuesto 18.21 por ciento\n           •       Los 67 programas no vinculados para reducir pobreza continúan en 2023 incrementando su presupuesto 10 por ciento.\n           •       De los 23 programas no vinculados por derechos en 2022, éstos continúan en 2023 aumentando su presupuesto 5.84 por ciento.\n If a patient can’t get needed medications at a local hospital, the patient, the patient’s doctor or the pharmacist would be able to call up the warehouse and get it delivered from the huge 40,000 square meter (430,000 square foot) Mexico City warehouse.\nThe armed forces, or the government-run pharmaceutical company Birmex, will ship the drugs out by land or air “within 24 to 48 hours,” López Obrador pledged.\nThe question is whether Mexico can overcome its history of being bad at regulating the pharmaceutical industry, bad at buying medicines, bad at storing them, and bad at distributing them. Extreme centralization also hasn’t helped Mexico much in the past in many areas.\nThe most visible face of this problem are the parents of children with cancer, who frequently stage protests because they say that in recent years chemotherapy and other drugs have been impossible to obtain.\n\nRelated Stories\nCuba loosens control of its private sector in a struggle with a mounting humanitarian crisis\n3 MIN READ\n13\n‘She killed the kids!’ 911 call from Lindsay Clancy’s husband brings tears to the courtroom\n4 MIN READ\n176\nFederal survey shows promising trends for youth mental health and drug use\n1 MIN READ\nDesperate parents blocked traffic at the Mexico City airport last year, holding up a banner reading: “There isn’t any chemotherapy, treatment or medicines, have some empathy and sensitivity.”\nRead More\nThe problems have killed otherwise healthy people. Because Mexico has had problems in obtaining enough morphine, anesthesiologists in Mexico have had to carry around their own vials of the sedative, drawing multiple doses out of a single vial for routine procedures like spinal blocks during births.\nIn the United States, where there is no shortage of morphine, doctors are advised to draw a single dose from a vial and throw the remainder out.\nBut in Mexico,\nthat has led to contamination of the vials,\ntriggering\noutbreaks of injection-induced meningitis in two Mexican states that have killed dozens of people\n— including some Americans who sought treatment at clinics in the border city of Matamoros, across from Brownsville, Texas.\nSign up for Morning Wire:\nOur flagship newsletter breaks down the biggest headlines of the day.\nEmail address\nSign up\nBy checking this box, you agree to AP's\nTerms of Use\nand acknowledge that AP may collect and use your data pursuant to our\n\nPrivacy Policy\n.\nLópez Obrador mounted a major effort to obtain COVID-19 vaccines in 2021, using the armed forces to distribute them and volunteers to help apply them, and by the end of that year just about anybody in Mexico who wanted a vaccine got one, for free.\nBut trying to replicate that model of centralized government purchasing and army distribution on a national scale for thousands of medications is not the same, according to Mauricio Rodríguez, a professor at the School of Medicine at Mexico’s National Autonomous University.\n“This is crazy,” said Rodríguez, noting the government is opening the centralized warehouse without answering how the system will operate, especially for urgently-needed medications. He noted that concentrating all the drugs at one site increases risks, and could sideline some already-existing distribution systems.\nMany of the problems pre-date López Obrador, who took office in late 2018. For decades, there have been scandals involving of millions of dollars worth of medicines going out-of-date at warehouses while hospitals couldn’t get them.\nThe country’s medicine regulatory agency, known by its Spanish acronym as\nCofepris, was already so riddled with corruption\nprior to López Obrador that regulators would hide applications for approval of new medicines for years, and demand bribes to approve them.\nAnd with alarming frequency, regulators in Mexico send out alerts about falsified or knock-off medications being sold for treating everything from cancer to heart disease. Boxes, labels, vials and certifications are copied with amazing accuracy, but the bottles often contain little or none of the medication.\nThe fake medicine trade is so common, and so lucrative in Mexico\nbecause patients or their relatives are often told by doctors to buy medications at private drug stores when they are unavailable at government hospitals.\nThe civic group “Zero Shortages” said there was an increase of 142% in the number of alerts about falsified medicines between 2021 and 2022.\nBut part of the problems are of López Obrador’s own making. Angry at what he claimed were inflated profits made by drug distributors and importers, the president simply cut the private companies out and decided the government should directly buy all medications.\nBecause the government did not have much infrastructure, contacts or experience in such a massive effort, López Obrador signed an agreement with the World Health Organization to help Mexico in purchasing. But even with that help, Mexico was unable to obtain some specialized medication, something López Obrador blamed on sabotage by pharmaceutical firms.\nRafael Gual, the director of the National Pharmaceutical Industry Chamber, said the government’s own actions created “bottlenecks” in distribution.\nAccording to the group “Zero Shortages,” the number of prescriptions that went unfilled in Mexico rose from 1.5 million in 2019 to 22 million in 2021; disruptions due to the COVID pandemic probably played a role.\nBut even in 2022, there were still about 12.5 million prescriptions that went unfilled.\nDr. José Moya, WHO’s Mexico representative, said centralized medical warehouses can be a solution, but the key is to have a good logistical system.\n“If they are considering a warehouse like this, it’s because there is a need,” Moya said, “and this has to be very well organized.”\nMARÍA VERZA\nVerza has focused on immigration, violence and human rights stories in Mexico and Central America for more than a decade\ntwitter\ninstagram\nmailto", "excerpt_sha256": "08c0072bdefb71e6245d121877e6cc0caa134ef36bce56093ab9e9c9bd8a816d", "verification_status": "corrected", "verification_notes": ["The excerpt does not explicitly state December 29, 2023; por lo que tengo a bien emitir el siguiente:\nACUERDO MEDIANTE EL CUAL SE ESTABLECEN LAS REGLAS DE OPERACIÓN DEL PROGRAMA\nIMSS-BIENESTAR PARA EL EJERCICIO FISCAL 2023\nÚNICO.-\nEl Instituto Mexicano del Seguro Social establece las Reglas de Operación del Programa\nIMSS-\nBIENESTAR aplicables durante el ejercicio fiscal 2023, las cuales se detallan en el Anexo del presente\nAcuerdo.\nTRANSITORIOS\nPRIMERO.-\nEl presente Acuerdo entrará en vigor el día 1 de enero del Ejercicio Fiscal 2023 con vigencia\nhasta el 31 de diciembre del mismo año.\nSEGUNDO.-\nLos indicadores de desempeño\n\n,\npodrán adecuarse, en su caso, en términos de los\ncomentarios emitidos por las instancias legalmente competentes. Los indicadores de cada Ejercicio\nFiscal\nestarán disponibles en la página electrónica del Instituto Mexicano del Seguro Social, en la sección\ncorrespondiente al Programa IMSS-BIENESTAR.\nTERCERO.-\nEl lenguaje empleado en las presentes Reglas de Operación no busca generar ninguna\ndistinción ni marcar diferencias entre hombres y mujeres, por lo que las referencias o alusiones en la\nredacción hechas hacia un género representan a ambos sexos. En la ejecución del Programa se incorporará\nla equidad y perspectiva de género, lenguaje incluyente y no sexista.\nCUARTO.-\nLas presentes Reglas de Operación son de observancia obligatoria en todo el ámbito de\noperación del Programa IMSS-BIENESTAR.\nQUINTO.-\nLa operación y actividades del Programa IMSS-BIENESTAR, están sujetas a la disponibilidad\npresupuestal anual que autoriza la H. Cámara de Diputados en el Presupuesto de Egresos de la Federación\ndel ejercicio fiscal correspondiente, así como a las sucesivas modificaciones que realice la Secretaría de\nHacienda y Crédito Público\n.\nSEXTO.-\nEl Modelo de Atención a la Salud para el Bienestar (MAS-BIENESTAR), publicado en el Diario\nOficial de la Federación el 25 de octubre de 2022, representa la base para la transformación operativa\nde los\nmodelos de atención para la prestación gratuita de servicios de salud, medicamentos y demás\n\ninsumos\nasociados para las personas sin seguridad social, bajo la responsabilidad de aquellas\ninstituciones y\nprogramas de salud que conforman el Sistema Nacional de Salud. En razón de lo anterior, el Programa IMSS-\nBIENESTAR, como parte de este Sistema, en coordinación con el organismo público descentralizado\ndenominado Servicios de Salud del Instituto Mexicano del Seguro Social para el Bienestar (IMSS-\nBIENESTAR), armonizará operativa y normativamente su actual modelo de atención (MAIS) conforme al\nnuevo modelo.\nCiudad de México, a 20 de diciembre de 2022.- El Director General del Instituto Mexicano del Seguro\nSocial,\nZoé Alejandro Robledo Aburto\n.- Rúbrica.\nANEXO\nREGLAS DE OPERACIÓN DEL PROGRAMA IMSS-BIENESTAR\nAPLICABLES DURANTE EL EJERCICIO\nFISCAL 2023\nContenido\n1.\nIntroducción\n1.1.\nGlosario de definiciones y acrónimos\n2.\nObjetivos\n2.1.\nGeneral", "excerpt_sha256": "15d1943935e630a5561f111b150bd5c200fee2bbb556c7df963e63dd94501e8f", "verification_status": "accepted", "verification_notes": []}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0051-E002", "source_id": "SRC-3c3762c63f8d8ebc", "url": "https://sidof.segob.gob.mx/notas/docFuente/5675919", "title": "Reglas de Operación del Programa IMSS-BIENESTAR para el ejercicio fiscal 2023", "publisher": "Diario Oficial de la Federación", "raw_sha256": "90aa8829a729a7dc4dca51c82c22794c4d5d3468f9dabe011b4ee6cfc2d0827b", "fact_summary": "IMSS-BIENESTAR’s rules described preventive, epidemiological, ambulatory, hospital, specialty, palliative and rehabilitation services, associated medicines, community participation and citizen oversight for people without social security. Objetivos\n2.1.\nGeneral\nContribuir al bienestar social e igualdad de las personas, otorgando atención a población sin seguridad\nsocial mediante un MAS-BIENESTAR que vincula los servicios de salud y la Acción Comunitaria.\nEl Programa desarrollará estos objetivos mediante los componentes de: Atención a la Salud y Acción\nComunitaria. Lo anterior, en un marco de respeto a los derechos humanos, con perspectiva de género y con\nEnfoque Intercultural en Salud a la población usuaria, con base en el MAS-BIENESTAR, que, a su vez, se\nsustenta en el Modelo de Atención Integral a la Salud (MAIS).\nCuadro 1.\nModelo de Atención a las Salud para el Bienestar (MAS-BIENESTAR)\n2.2.\nEspecíficos\n2.2.1.\nRealizar las acciones de prevención, vigilancia epidemiológica y salud pública de acuerdo con el\nPrograma Sectorial de Salud 2020-2024 y con el Programa Estratégico de Salud para el Bienestar.\n2.2.2.\nOtorgar servicios de salud ambulatorios y hospitalarios, de especialidad, paliativos y de\nrehabilitación, así como los medicamentos asociados, a la población sin seguridad social, a través de sus\nunidades de salud y hospitales, con Enfoque Intercultural en Salud\n.\n2.2.3.\n\nImplementar estrategias comunitarias para impulsar la participación a nivel individual, familiar y\ncomunitario en acciones de promoción en el autocuidado de la salud; identificación y derivación oportuna a\npersonas con necesidades de atención médica; así como apoyar en la atención a aquellas que requieran los\nservicios de los albergues comunitarios tutelados por el Programa\n.\n2.2.4.\nFomentar la participación ciudadana y la contraloría social, a través del Aval Ciudadano, con la\nfinalidad de dar seguimiento a la calidad en la prestación de los servicios médicos, conocer la satisfacción de\nlos usuarios, sus inconformidades, quejas, sugerencias y felicitaciones, o en su caso, formular propuestas\npara la mejora continua de los establecimientos médicos del Programa.\n3. proceso de registro\n.\nEl número de unidades de salud y hospitales puede presentar variaciones en el transcurso del año 2023,\nen virtud de la dinámica operativa, por incorporación de nueva infraestructura en los OOAD por asignación o\ndonación de inmuebles en favor del Programa, o bien, derivado de la baja de unidades de salud o\nmodificaciones en su tipología\n.\nEl número de camas la\nUnidad del Programa.\nAdicionalmente, el Programa despliega estrategias o acciones encaminadas a la promoción de la salud,\ncomplementarias de salud pública o de índole comunitario.\n3.1.1.\n\nUniverso de trabajo\nLocalidades que se ubican en el área geográfica que atiende el Programa y comprende la localidad sede\nde la unidad de salud y las localidades de acción intensiva (LAI).\n3.1.2.\nCobertura por Zona de Servicios Médicos\nLa Zona de Servicios Médicos está conformada por un hospital\ny una o más zonas de supervisión, la cual\nconstituye la unidad funcional para prestar los servicios de salud con el objetivo de vincular la continuidad de\nla atención de los pacientes dentro de una RISS.\nLa Zona de Servicios Médicos tiene un promedio de cobertura poblacional de 150 mil personas. Dicha\ncobertura podrá variar conforme a las condiciones geográficas, la facilidad de acceso a los servicios para la\npoblación, así como a la posible extensión de los servicios de salud por parte del Programa.\nLa cobertura promedio de población a atender de un hospital en su localidad sede va de 15 a 30 mil\npersonas, mientras que para una unidad fija de salud es de aproximadamente 3 mil personas.\nEn el caso de las unidades itinerantes, éstas prestan servicios de salud mediante rutas establecidas a\npoblaciones geográficamente dispersas.\nCorresponde al personal de salud del Programa estabilizar, derivar y orientar a los pacientes y sus familias\npara acceder a servicios de salud de mayor complejidad, cuando se rebase la capacidad resolutiva de la\noferta de servicios que proporciona el Programa.\n3.1.3.\nAmpliación de cobertura\nEl Programa se suma a la política pública del Sector Salud implementada por el Gobierno Federal, para lo\ncual, colaborará de manera articulada con IMSS-BIENESTAR, en la implementación de la estrategia de\nfederalización, conforme a los convenios que éste celebre con las entidades federativas, para que con base\nen el MAS-BIENESTAR se amplíe la cobertura de servicios de salud a la población sin seguridad social\n\ncontribuyendo así a garantizar el acceso universal y gratuito de estos servicios, a fin de coadyuvar en la\nconstrucción del Sistema Único de Salud para el Bienestar.\n3.1.4.\nLocalidad sede\nEs aquella en la que opera una unidad de salud del Programa. Cuando las localidades cuentan con\ninfraestructura de más de una institución de salud, el área geográfica se divide en sectores de responsabilidad\npor acuerdo interinstitucional.\n3.1.5.\nLocalidad de Atención Intensiva (LAI)\nLocalidad aledaña a una unidad de salud del Programa, cuyos habitantes reciben servicios a través de\nalguno de sus componentes, apoyados principalmente por los grupos voluntarios de la organización\ncomunitaria para la salud.\n3.1.6.\nÁrea de influencia\nLocalidades que no pertenecen al universo de trabajo del Programa cuyos habitantes demandan servicios\na través de alguno de sus componentes.\n3.2.\nPoblación\n3.2.1.\nPoblación potencial\nPersonas sin seguridad social en el país.\n3.2.2.\nPoblación objetivo\nPersonas sin seguridad social que residen en localidades del universo de trabajo. Esta población se\nencuentra inscrita en el PPA del Programa.\n\n3.2.3.\nPoblación atendida\nAquella que es beneficiada por alguna de las acciones en salud del Programa y está inscrita en el PPA.\n3.3.\nBeneficiarios\nEs aquella persona que ha recibido algún servicio derivado de las atenciones que proporciona el\nPrograma. Los datos personales de los beneficiarios serán registrados en el PPA.\nLos servicios del Programa están dirigidos a la población sin seguridad social, con el único requisito de\nregistrarse en el PPA mediante la presentación de su CURP o acta de nacimiento e identificación oficial.\n3.3.1.\nPadrón de Población Adscrita\nEl Programa cuenta con un PPA que es una base de información construida a partir del levantamiento de\ninformación de la población, la cual se actualiza de manera permanente en términos de lo dispuesto en el\nprocedimiento normativo institucional; y es sometida a revisiones periódicas de acuerdo con la disponibilidad\npresupuestal del Programa.\nEl\nPPA incorpora una lista nominal de la población que cumple alguna de las siguientes condiciones:\ni) Habita en el universo de trabajo y no cuenta con seguridad social.\nii) Habita en el universo de trabajo, cuenta con acceso a algún sistema de seguridad social y se le han\notorgado servicios a través de algún componente del Programa.\niii) Habita en el área de influencia y sin importar su condición de aseguramiento se le otorga servicio\nrecurrente a través de algún componente del Programa.\niv)\nMigrante extranjero y se le otorga servicio a través de algún componente del Programa durante su\ntránsito por el territorio nacional.", "excerpt_sha256": "13fb13d3821fe4aa4a730f71f6d9813aabec3e308ac0457670259a2e8c2212f4", "verification_status": "corrected", "verification_notes": ["The excerpt lists 4,075, not merely a count of hospitals and health units independently verified in 2023.", "The text describes intended or authorized arrangements and a proposed federalization strategy, not completed expansion or effective universal coverage."]}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0051-E004", "source_id": "SRC-3c3762c63f8d8ebc", "url": "https://sidof.segob.gob.mx/notas/docFuente/5675919", "title": "Reglas de Operación del Programa IMSS-BIENESTAR para el ejercicio fiscal 2023", "publisher": "Diario Oficial de la Federación", "raw_sha256": "90aa8829a729a7dc4dca51c82c22794c4d5d3468f9dabe011b4ee6cfc2d0827b", "fact_summary": "The rules prescribed multidisciplinary supervision and advisory visits, written commitments, performance and quality reviews, corrective measures, internal and external evaluations, and public reporting of external evaluation results. mecanismo de supervisión, asesoría y seguimiento,\nestableciendo compromisos y acuerdos con base en resultados, a los cuales dará seguimiento.\nA su vez el personal de conducción del Programa en los OOAD, serán los responsables de realizar dicho\nproceso en su ámbito de responsabilidad, estableciendo compromisos y acuerdos a los cuales dará\nseguimiento.\nEsta supervisión, asesoría y seguimiento, deberá tener carácter multidisciplinario, optimizando tiempo y\nrecursos, colaborará en el cumplimiento de los objetivos, indicadores y metas, con el propósito de facilitar la\ntoma de decisiones, detectar oportunamente desviaciones operativas o de conducción y adoptar, en su caso,\nmedidas correctivas e implementar acciones de mejora continua, a efecto de dar seguimiento y ponderar visitas, para brindar asesoría,\ncomprobar, instruir y apoyar el desarrollo y ejecución de las prioridades en salud, así como valorar su grado\nde avance y el cumplimiento de los objetivos de estas niveles de atención a su cargo, incluyendo\nprocesos acordes a las prioridades, estrategias, componentes del Programa y los aspectos técnico médico\nadministrativos. Esta supervisión se realizará mediante la aplicación de cédulas de supervisión elaboradas por\nla Unidad del Programa, las cuales incluyen el desempeño directivo y evaluación de la calidad de la atención,\nse elaborarán compromisos específicos acordes a las cédulas de supervisión y normatividad, con registro en\nlas bitácoras de cada unidad de salud de primer nivel supervisada; para el caso de los hospitales se debe\nutilizar el formato de compromisos establecido por la Unidad del Programa\n.\nPlan de Supervisión, Asesoría y Seguimiento del Nivel Operativo del Programa,\nen relación con el cual los\nintegrantes de los EGAS, EMS, Cuerpos de Gobierno y ESAZ realizarán visitas de supervisión y asesoría\nintegral, de manera multidisciplinaria, a las unidades de salud de los diferentes niveles de atención a su cargo,\nincluyendo procesos acordes a las prioridades, estrategias, componentes del Programa y los aspectos técnico\nmédico administrativos. Esta supervisión se realizará mediante la aplicación de cédulas de supervisión que\nincluye desempeño directivo y evaluación de la calidad de la atención, se elaborarán compromisos específicos\n\ncon registro en las bitácoras de cada unidad de salud de primer nivel supervisada; para el caso de los\nhospitales se debe utilizar el formato de compromisos establecido por la Unidad.\nLos EGAS deben supervisar un hospital y dos unidades de salud de primer nivel por mes. El EMS debe\nvisitar un hospital y al menos cinco unidades de salud de primer nivel por mes.\nLos Cuerpos de Gobierno de Hospital deben programar visitas conjuntas al 100% de las áreas y servicios,\ndurante los bimestres febrero-marzo y julio-agosto, independientemente de la supervisión individual. El ESAZ\ndebe supervisar el 100% de las unidades de salud bajo su responsabilidad al menos cada dos meses.\nNo se considerarán como visitas de supervisión la aplicación parcial de las cédulas de supervisión, sin\nbase normativa y/o sin la participación integral multidisciplinaria de acuerdo con el nivel de conducción.\n3.5.4.\nDerechos de los beneficiarios\nLos beneficiarios del Programa tendrán derecho en los establecimientos médicos a:\n·\nRecibir atención médica gratuita, con igualdad y sin discriminación.\n·\nRecibir un trato digno y respetuoso.\n·\nRecibir información sobre su proceso de atención, suficiente, clara, oportuna y veraz.\n·\nDecidir libremente sobre su atención.\n·\nOtorgar o no su consentimiento válidamente informado.\n·\nSer tratado con confidencialidad.\n·\n\nContar con facilidades para obtener una segunda opinión.\n·\nRecibir atención médica en caso de urgencia.\n·\nContar con un Expediente Clínico.\n·\nSer atendido cuando se inconforme con los servicios recibidos.\n·\nRecibir atención médica libre de riesgos mediante procedimientos seguros.\nPara hacer efectivos estos derechos, las unidades de salud y hospitales del Programa contarán con un\nSistema de Atención Ciudadana, para captar las quejas, sugerencias y felicitaciones, así como con\nprocedimientos que incluyan su análisis regular por los Equipos de Conducción y el COCASEP, con la\nparticipación del Aval Ciudadano, y siendo turnadas y atendidas por las distintas áreas en el ámbito de sus\nresponsabilidades y competencias.\n3.6.\nInstancias normativas y participantes\nLa operación del Programa está regulada por la legislación federal aplicable, las presentes Reglas de\nOperación, Normas Oficiales Mexicanas (NOM), concernientes a la Cartera de Servicios del Programa,\nProtocolos de Atención emitidos por la Secretaría de Salud, documentos normativos del IMSS que le resulten\naplicables, Procedimientos, Guías, Lineamientos y oficios circulares emitidos por la Unidad del Programa.\n3.6.1.\nInstancia normativa\nCorresponde a la Unidad del Programa la definición de los aspectos que regulan su ejecución. Tendrá las\nsiguientes facultades:\nI.\n\nOrganizar la administración y operación de los dos componentes del Programa, en sus diferentes\nniveles de conducción y operación, en colaboración articulada con IMSS-BIENESTAR, atendiendo\na los objetivos, alineados al Programa Estratégico de Salud para el Bienestar y en términos de los\nConvenios de Colaboración que, para tal efecto, suscriba el IMSS con el IMSS-BIENESTAR\n;\nII.\nSometer los asuntos de su competencia a consideración del Director General y Órganos\nNormativos del IMSS, según sea el caso, para el eficaz desempeño de las atribuciones que les\nconfieren las Leyes, el RIIMSS y demás disposiciones reglamentarias y administrativas;\nIII.\nSuscribir convenios de colaboración y acuerdos interinstitucionales, con los diversos órdenes de\nGobierno, organismos públicos y no gubernamentales; para el cumplimiento de prioridades de\natención en favor de la salud de las personas sin seguridad social\n;\nIV.\nRealizar los procesos administrativos alineados con IMSS-BIENESTAR conforme a los convenios\nde colaboración que para tal efecto suscriba con el IMSS;\nV.\nPlanear y organizar la participación del Programa, en aquellos programas o estrategias federales\nde salud;\nVI.\nAlinear el Programa con el Programa Sectorial de Salud, el Programa Estratégico de Salud para el\nBienestar y el Programa Institucional del Instituto Mexicano del Seguro Social vigentes\n;\nVII.\n\nDesignar a los integrantes de la estructura de conducción del Programa,\nadscritos a los OOAD.\nVIII.\nAcordar con la Unidad de Educación e Investigación del IMSS las estrategias, cobertura, desarrollo\ny evaluación del personal becario que realizan su servicio social y médicos residentes en periodo\nde adiestramiento, dentro de las unidades de salud\ndel Programa;\nIX.\nOrganizar el programa de trabajo para la certificación de los establecimientos médicos del\nPrograma ante el Consejo General de Salud.\nX.\nAprobar las acciones para la operación de\nla Contraloría Social y Aval Ciudadano en el Programa.\nXI.\nAprobar los montos asignados para el otorgamiento del apoyo económico y en especie a los\nintegrantes de la Organización Comunitaria del Programa;\nXII.\nDeterminar y aprobar los Sistemas de Información del Programa y la aplicación del PPA;\nXIII.\nAdministrar, en coordinación con las Direcciones de Finanzas, de Administración y los OOAD, los\nrecursos para la operación del Programa;\nXIV.\nCoordinar con las Direcciones Normativas, los OOAD y con IMSS-BIENESTAR, las sinergias\nnecesarias para que, a través de la infraestructura, estructura organizacional y sistemas de\ninformación del IMSS, éste lleve a cabo la ejecución del Programa\n\n;\nXV.\nProponer, y en su caso, aprobar el anteproyecto de Presupuesto de operación e inversión del\nPrograma, correspondiente al ejercicio fiscal siguiente;\nXVI.\nLa Unidad del Programa es la única instancia normativa para proponer, y en su caso, aprobar los\nproyectos en materia de obra pública para la sustitución, ampliación y remodelación de la\ninfraestructura médica en el ámbito de operación del Programa; así como los programas de\nequipamiento relacionados con los proyectos en materia de obra pública, así como los programas\nde equipamiento de la infraestructura médica ya existente y por incorporarse\n;\nXVII.\nAcordar conjuntamente con los órganos normativos del IMSS, las condiciones de trabajo del\npersonal del Programa con tipo de contratación base y confianza, así como al que alude el\nEstatuto a que se refiere el artículo 286 I de la Ley del Seguro Social\n;\nXVIII.\nAprobar las estrategias para la formación, capacitación y educación permanente del personal del\nPrograma;\nXIX.\nDifundir y utilizar los resultados obtenidos de las evaluaciones interna y externa del Programa, con\nel fin de analizar su operación, avances e impacto, así como definir las acciones de mejora\nnecesarias, transparentar la operación y el uso correcto de los recursos asignados;\nXX.\nSancionar la instrumentación y aplicación de los lineamientos y procedimientos en materia de\n\nabastecimiento de insumos, equipamiento, contratación de servicios, construcción de obra pública,\nconservación de bienes muebles e inmuebles y seguridad en los inmuebles, a fin de cubrir las\nnecesidades operativas de las unidades de salud Instituto.\n3.6.2.\nInstancias participantes\nCon el propósito de garantizar la aplicación del MAS-BIENESTAR a la población beneficiaria del Programa\ny verificar la aplicación de las estrategias, Niveles de Conducción del Programa\nNivel de conducción del\nPrograma\nÁmbito\nFunciones esenciales\nTitular de la Unidad y\nCoordinadores\nNacional\no\nPlaneación y metas.\no", "excerpt_sha256": "360563149e6e4f9c1d5a83f5b12ba1ec29b0a5212ed26dcc3be2890e3296c121", "verification_status": "corrected", "verification_notes": ["The excerpt supports formal authority and mechanisms, but not independent evaluations or demonstrated effectiveness."]}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0051-E014", "source_id": "SRC-cb7adb2d19e644c1", "url": "https://csis-website-prod.s3.amazonaws.com/s3fs-public/2023-07/230718_Berg_After_AMLO_0.pdf", "title": "After AMLO: The Economic, Security, and Political Outlook for Mexico in 2024", "publisher": "Center for Strategic and International Studies", "raw_sha256": "112c3fb2368e464f70403bf4f07af82fb3b49ec04385a56a31a037931a4b98bd", "fact_summary": "López Obrador halted new foreign oil contracts, audited more than 100 previous awards for corruption and committed billions to the Dos Bocas refinery, but the refinery was reported substantially over budget. His proposed electricity reform would have strengthened state control and subordinated autonomous regulators; Congress failed to approve it, limiting his ability to enact the full agenda. Lithium nationalization and hostility to foreign energy investment raised concerns about investor confidence and clean-energy transition.", "question_ids": ["5B.1", "5B.10", "5B.3", "5B.4", "5B.5", "5B.6", "8B.10", "8B.2", "8B.6", "8B.7", "8B.9"], "polarity": "mixed", "period_fit": "The assessment covers policies and legislative outcomes through 2023.", "ruler_attribution": "The decisions and legislative initiative were attributed to López Obrador’s administration.", "limitations": ["The report includes forecasts and estimates as well as completed policy actions."], "start_unit": 8, "end_unit": 9, "locator": "page 8 through page 9", "exact_excerpt": "Energy Reform\nPresident López Obrador undoubtedly has a keen appreciation for the centrality of energy to Mexican politics\nand civil society. Indeed, he was carried into power in no small part by the outpouring of popular discontent\nagainst the Peña Nieto administration in 2017 as protests erupted against rising gas prices. The spike in\ngasoline prices, known as a gasolinazo, was the sharpest rise in Mexico in two decades.8 Overall, prices in\nsome areas rose by 48 percent as the government under President Enrique Peña Nieto passed a major energy\nreform that ended the legal monopoly of Petróleos Mexicanos (Pemex), Mexico’s state-owned oil company.\nPemex holds the dubious title of the world’s most indebted state-run oil firm and is widely reported to lack\nthe funds or technology to fully develop Mexico’s oil reserves (the country ranks 21st in the world in terms of\ncrude oil resources).9 Nevertheless, the dramatic effect of the gasolinazo protests served to reinforce lessons\nthat López Obrador, a long-standing opponent of privatization, had already taken to heart: the energy reforms\nof his predecessor must be rolled back, and gas prices must remain stable.\nAMLO quickly set about working to realize these goals, placing a hold on new contracts for foreign oil\ncompanies and auditing over 100 of the awards signed under Peña Nieto for corruption.10 At the same time,\nin the early months of his presidency, AMLO declared an $8 billion investment for a new Pemex refinery to be\nlocated in his home state of Tabasco, a commitment later supplemented with a $600 million investment from\nChinese state banks.11 Expected to become operational in 2023, the refinery project has already run some $3\nbillion over budget.12\nView of the Mexican oil company PEMEX’s oil complex in Tula, Hidalgo state, Mexico, on January 11, 2018.\nPhoto Source: Pedro Pardo/AFP/Getty Images\nBy far the most ambitious project the president has undertaken is a proposed constitutional reform that would\nsignificantly strengthen the state’s role in the energy sector. Under the proposed law, Mexico’s state-owned\npower company, the Comisión Federal de Electricidad (CFE), would have a guaranteed 54 percent market\nshare while limiting the participation of private electricity suppliers. It would also fold the autonomous Energy\nRegulatory Commission (CRE) and the National Hydrocarbons Commission (CNH) into the federal government\nby making them subordinate to the Ministry of Energy.13\n4 | Ryan C. Berg, Sara Fattori, and Henry Ziemer\n\nThe amendment proved a rallying cry for domestic opposition, which cited inefficiencies within the CFE that,\nby some estimates, would increase operating costs by as much as $3 billion a year while increasing the risk\nof power outages by as much as 30 percent.14 The amendment also sparked major concerns internationally,\nespecially among U.S. energy firms, which voiced fears about the stability of their previous investments.\nUltimately, AMLO was unable to muster the political capital to move the initiative forward, as the lower house\nof Congress failed to reach the two-thirds supermajority needed to advance a constitutional amendment.15 The\nfailure of the reform showcased hard limits on AMLO’s power to drive sweeping legislative change in critical\neconomic sectors, providing a greater degree of clarity for prospective investors and businesses.\nHowever, the defeat of the energy reform amendment was far from an unmitigated victory for opponents. In\nApril 2022, Mexico’s Supreme Court issued an ambiguous decision on an earlier energy law that gave the CFE\npriority access to send its energy to the grid before foreign energy producers.16 Ultimately, AMLO’s handling of\nenergy policy impelled the United States and Canada to seek joint consultations under the USMCA in July 2022.\nThus far, the question of energy policy has proved a thorny issue, with the specter of a dispute resolution\npanel raising lingering questions for Mexico’s domestic industry and external investors alike. It also threatens\nto stall nearshoring momentum.\nHigh energy prices coupled with the CFE’s heavy dependency on oil for power generation mean companies\nstruggle to obtain cost-effective power and meet their environmental, social, and corporate governance (ESG)\ntargets while investing in Mexico. Furthermore, in the wake of the amendment’s defeat, AMLO appears to have\nshifted his attention toward the nationalization of other industries. On April 17, the same day as the vote in the\nlower house, the president introduced a reform to the mining law to nationalize Mexico’s lithium industry. The\nlaw was fast-tracked through Congress and approved just two days later.17\nTHE ENERGY OUTLOOK FOR 2024\nThe nationalization of lithium will likely set Mexico back in the transition to lower carbon emissions and the\npush for climate-smart energy solutions. When coupled with AMLO’s hostility to foreign energy companies,\nmany of which seek to invest in renewable power sources, Mexico has locked itself into a traditional carbon-\nand-oil energy strategy for the near future. Indeed, Pemex is a mainstay of AMLO’s fourth transformation\ndomestic project. Mexico’s ability to diversify this strategy in 2024 will depend, in part, on the extent to which\ninvestor confidence can be restored by resolving Mexico’s energy uncertainty through USMCA arbitration and\nthe willingness of AMLO to strenuously oppose future foreign investments in the energy sector.18 Not only does\nthis policy impair Mexico’s ability to meet clean energy and environmental goals, it also reverses one of the most\npositive trends to come out of the 2013 reform, which prompted a windfall of investment in renewable energy\nindustries.19 Lithium may buck this trend but only if the national lithium company proves capable of rapidly and\nefficiently scaling up production, an unlikely prospect as only one lithium mining project, the Chinese-owned\nBacanora Lithium, appears to stand a realistic chance of beginning production by the end of 2023.20\nAnother key concern in the near term is gasoline price stability at a time of rising global oil prices. While\nAMLO will likely do everything in his power to avoid a gasolinazo under his administration, his energy policies\nwill become increasingly untenable over the medium and long term as the administration pours more funds\ninto subsidized fuel. A deterioration of dispute resolution procedures under the USMCA will only exacerbate\nsuch a downward trend, potentially exposing Mexico to punitive tariffs and stifling necessary investment\nin sectors needed for the energy transition. Thus, AMLO may successfully stave off an energy crisis under\nhis administration, but absent a significant course correction when a future crisis hits, it may produce even\ngreater disruptions than those observed in 2017.\n5 | After AMLO: The Economic, Security, and Political Outlook for Mexico in 2024", "excerpt_sha256": "1102c7f963b73f9548d67bfe409b9ce0ad3b90c4c2f60d0df073ad6167accac3", "verification_status": "accepted", "verification_notes": []}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0051-E017", "source_id": "SRC-cb7adb2d19e644c1", "url": "https://csis-website-prod.s3.amazonaws.com/s3fs-public/2023-07/230718_Berg_After_AMLO_0.pdf", "title": "After AMLO: The Economic, Security, and Political Outlook for Mexico in 2024", "publisher": "Center for Strategic and International Studies", "raw_sha256": "112c3fb2368e464f70403bf4f07af82fb3b49ec04385a56a31a037931a4b98bd", "fact_summary": "CSIS reported about 34,000 homicides in 2021, described 2022 as the most violent year recorded by the National Public Security System, reported 100 political assassinations during the 2021 midterm elections, and stated that criminal organizations had expanded control to claim up to 40 percent of Mexican territory. Each year, Mexico experiences around\n30,000 murders, which have risen to record levels during AMLO’s mandate.60 In 2021 the nation\nregistered 34,000 homicides, double the number in 2015, while 2022 was the most violent year ever recorded\nby the National Public Security System.61 According to Bloomberg, Mexico remains home to eight of the world’s\nmost dangerous cities.62 Insecurity also characterizes the political scene: in the 2021 midterm elections, 100\npolitical assassinations were reported, 35 of which targeted political candidates.63 With a year and a half\nremaining in AMLO’s term, homicides on his watch have already equaled those under his predecessor’s\nsix-year term and have surpassed by 22 percent the total during the six-year term of Felipe Calderón, the\npresident whose war on drugs AMLO has derided. Simply put, AMLO’s sexenio will be the bloodiest six-year\nterm in Mexican history.64\nViolence associated with conflict against drug cartels claims most of these victims. López Obrador’s “abrazos,\nno balazos” (hugs, not bullets) security policy has seen criminal organizations expand their control to claim up\nto 40 percent of Mexican territory.65 With Mexico experiencing some of the highest levels of extortion, human\ntrafficking, human rights violations, and femicides registered in the country’s history, Mexico’s situation under\nAMLO displays an alarming trend.66 These patterns present no signs of slowing, as AMLO’s security reforms\nhave reduced public spending on security, muddied the citizen security landscape, and reduced cooperation\nwith the United States on security and counternarcotics issues.67\n13 | After AMLO: The Economic, Security, and Political Outlook for Mexico in 2024\n\nMembers of the Mexican army drive on a road a day after an operation to arrest the son of Joaquín “El Chapo”\nGuzmán, Ovidio Guzmán, in Culiacán, Sinaloa state, Mexico, on January 6, 2023.\nPhoto Source: Juan Carlos Cruz/AFP/Getty Imges\nOrganized Crime\nAmong the many challenges Mexico faces, organized crime remains one of the most complex and resilient\nthreats the country has yet to find a successful strategy for addressing. In 2015 there were around 25 cartels\nrecognized as major criminal groupings—or what the U.S. Department of Justice calls “well-articulated”\ncriminal groups. By 2019 this figure had grown to 37, which is likely a significant underestimate.68 In fact,\nindependent observers identified the presence of up to 463 criminal entities on Mexican territory, explaining\nthat criminal groupings have grown ever smaller following internal fragmentation and the intensification\nof violence as a result of the government’s targeting of kingpins.69 At the same time, an underworld\nso fragmented and difficult to trace makes it even harder for political leadership to find adequate and\neffective measures to counter its activity, resulting in a vicious cycle. Some of these groups, such as Jalisco\nNew Generation Cartel (CJNG) or the Sinaloa Cartel, are powerful enough to challenge the state, and the\ntransformation and fragmentation they provoke either within or between each other often causes deadly\nepisodes of violence.70 Indeed, U.S. Drug Enforcement Administration (DEA) administrator Anne Milgram has\nstated that these two groups pose “the greatest criminal threat” ever faced by the United States.71\nDuring the pandemic, the role of criminal groups became twofold. On the one hand, they exacerbated insecurity\nas violent attacks on civilians and battles with other groups continued apace. On the other, criminal groups\ncomplemented, or in some cases took on, the role of the state by distributing food and aid and imposing a curfew\nto limit the spread of Covid-19. This was the case for the Sinaloa Cartel, which distributed “Chapo dispensas,”\nor boxes of supplies with erstwhile cartel leader Joaquín “El Chapo” Guzmán’s face emblazoned on them.72\nGovernment restrictions on mobility and tighter border controls also affected their activity, and as a result new\nchannels of criminality arose, such as cybercrimes, which are harder for authorities to detect.73 High profits\nand low risks for activities such as fraud, piracy, and cybertheft quickly attracted drug cartels and generated\n14 | Ryan C. INTRODUCTION\n1. This Performance and Learning Review (PLR) assesses the implementation of the World Bank\nGroup FY20-25 Country Partnership Framework (CPF) for the United Mexican States.1 The CPF was\ndiscussed by the Board on February 27, 2020, at the outset of the COVID-19 pandemic. Covering six years, it\naims to support three interlinked goals: (a) more rapid, more inclusive growth; (b) stronger institutions for\npublic finance, service delivery, and economic inclusion; and (c) sustainable infrastructure and climate action.\n2. The objectives of this PLR are to assess progress in CPF implementation and propose adjustments\nfor the remainder of the CPF period. The PLR has been prepared in 2023 at the mid-point of the CPF period,\nand benefits from updated knowledge about country context, program implementation, and lessons learned.\nIt also suggests forward-looking changes to the results framework, implementation, and risk management.\nThe PLR will inform the engagement with the new government to be elected in June 2024, the Completion\nand Learning Review (CLR) in FY25, and a new CPF planned for FY26.\n3. Immediately after CPF discussion, global and domestic crises hit Mexico, shifting demand for World\nBank collaboration, and affecting CPF implementation and graduation prospects. The COVID-19 pandemic\nresulted in a dramatic economic contraction of 8 percent of Gross Domestic Product (GDP) in 2020,2\ncompounded by a global recession and the economic fallout from Russia’s invasion of Ukraine. Although\nMexico continued to access external capital markets on reasonable terms, crisis management altered\npriorities and affected reforms. This undermined CPF implementation and Mexico’s graduation from the\nInternational Bank for Reconstruction and Development (IBRD).3 A shift in client demand for World Bank\nsupport led to a large increase in countercyclical IBRD policy financing versus the previous CPF period at the\nexpense of a fall in demand for investment lending. Several Investment Policy Financing (IPF) operations were\ncanceled or restructured at client request. The World Bank’s analytical engagement helped maintain\ncollaboration through several high-impact reports, which supported the policy content of the Development\nPolicy Financing (DPF) program. Nevertheless, as a result of these two fundamental global and domestic\nshifts, out of the 24 CPF objective indicators, eight have been achieved, one partially achieved, five are on\ntrack, and ten are off track, five of which are unlikely to be achieved.4\n4. This PLR confirms the original strategic direction and the duration of the 2020 CPF but proposes\nadjustments to its objectives and results framework in light of these dual shifts. The PLR confirms the three\nCPF Focus Areas5 but reduces the number of objectives from seven to six. The changes to the results\nframework reflect current reform space, client demand, and implementation capacity. The PLR also raises\nthe overall risk rating from “Moderate” to “Substantial.” The adjustments proposed in the PLR are aligned\nwith World Bank reforms currently under discussion, notably the Evolution Roadmap, which includes an\nelevated strategic role for middle-income countries on the global challenges agenda.6 The PLR thus\nsummarizes what the World Bank is doing in selected global challenges, particularly those addressed more\ndirectly in Focus Area C (Climate Change Adaptation and Mitigation; Energy Access; and Water Security and\nAccess) and identifies further opportunities for one World Bank Group (WBG) collaboration, through\ncoordination and deployment of collective knowledge and complementary instruments for greater impact.\n1 Country Partnership Framework, World Bank Report No. 137429-MX.\n2 National Statistics Institute (INEGI)’s monthly employment survey (ENOE) by July 2020.\n3 During preparation, the Graduation Discussion Income (GDI) provided a strong filter for the selection of the activities in the CPF.\n4 These terms are defined in Section III. Summary of Program Implementation and Annex 3.\n5 World Bank, “Mexico Systematic Country Diagnostic,” August 2019.\n6 World Bank, “Evolving the World Bank Group’s Mission, Operations, and Resources: A Roadmap,” December 2022, “Ending Poverty\non a Livable Planet: Report to Governors on World Bank Evolution,” September 2023, including the eight global challenges: (i) Climate\nChange Adaptation and Mitigation; (ii) Fragility and Conflict; (iii) Pandemic Prevention and Preparedness; (iv) Energy Access; (v) Food\nand Nutrition Security; (vi) Water Security and Access; (vii) Enabling Digitalization; and (viii) Protecting Biodiversity and Nature.\n1\n\nII. MAIN CHANGES IN COUNTRY CONTEXT\n5. The CPF was prepared shortly after the current administration assumed power and is aligned with\nthe National Development Plan. President Andrés Manuel López Obrador assumed power in December 2018\nand CPF preparation was completed in 2019. The government consolidated its support in the mid-term\nelections held in June 2021 and local elections in June 2023. Ahead of the next presidential elections in June\n2024, the World Bank is preparing its transition engagement,7 which the PLR will inform.\n6. Immediately after the CPF discussion in 2020, the global COVID-19 pandemic hit. Latin America and\nthe Caribbean (LAC) was the hardest hit region of the world in both health and economic terms. In Mexico,\nthe pandemic resulted in 600,000 estimated excess deaths in 2020, followed by about 800,000 in 2021.8 In\n2020, output suffered its largest collapse since the 1930s. This contraction was driven by social distancing\nmeasures, supply chain disruptions, and risk aversion on the part of consumers and tourists.9 Labor poverty\nincreased from 39.0 percent at end-2019 to 46.0 percent in the same quarter of 2020 (a historical peak due\nto the pandemic), falling back to 37.3 percent by third quarter of 2023. Monetary poverty rates (using the\nofficial poverty line - línea de pobreza por ingresos) showed a similar inverted-U trend going from 49.9\npercent in 2018 to 52.8 in 2020 followed by a rapid decline to 43.5 in 2020.10\n7. Other structural challenges persist. There are significant disparities across states and population\ngroups in Mexico,11 including gender, ethnicity and migration disparities. 12 Income inequality is high. Mexico\nhas a higher Gini coefficient than countries with similar GDP/head13 but the Gini coefficient for household\nincome has declined from 48.6 to 43.1 between 2016 and 2022 due to solid household income growth among\npoorer households. The digital divide risks further entrenching historical disparities in economic\nopportunity.14 Increased violence and insecurity driven by organized crime activities has deterred\ninvestment, reducing the scope to further address economic disparities.15\n8. The government focused on ensuring a solid macroeconomic framework during the pandemic. The\nadministration re-prioritized expenditures to focus on universal cash transfers to the elderly and public\ninvestment in hallmark infrastructure projects. Total public expenditure, at around 27 percent of GDP in 2020,\nexceeded pre-pandemic levels. This was funded by trust funds and tax settlements with large companies.\nThe government now renews its focus on several flagship investments, including (i) the reform of the National\nGuard, (ii) the interoceanic logistic corridor of the Isthmus of Tehuantepec, (iii) full operation of the Dos Bocas\nrefinery and progress on energy self-sufficiency, and (iv) labor market reforms.16\n9. GDP has recovered to pre-pandemic levels and the macroeconomic framework remains stable.\nMexico’s potential growth remains subdued on account of low productivity growth and tamed investment.17\nFollowing a mild recession in 2019, the economic contraction accelerated in 2020 with the pandemic-induced\n7 Mexico Electoral and Transition Engagement Advisory Services and Analytics (ASA) (P501990).\n8 Palacio-Mejía, L. “Leading causes of excess mortality in Mexico during the COVID-19 pandemic 2020-2021,” The Lancet, June 2022.\n9 The size of the sector as a share of GDP decreased from 8.6 percent in 2019 to an estimated 6.8 percent in 2020.\n10 CONEVAL: https://www.coneval.org.mx/Medicion/Paginas/ITLP-IS_pobreza_laboral.aspx and\nhttps://www.coneval.org.mx/Medicion/MP/Paginas/Pobreza_2022.aspx.\n11 In 2016, for instance, the average GDP per capita of one of the richest states (Nuevo León) was close to the average in Poland,\nwhile that of the poorest state (Chiapas) was similar to that of Honduras.\n12 World Bank, Macro and Poverty Outlook, fall 2023 http://macropovertyoutlook.worldbank.org. World Bank, “Female Labor Force\nParticipation in Mexico,” March 2021, World Bank, “World Development Report 2023: Migrants, refuges, and societies,” 2023.\n13 Mexico has the highest Gini against comparison countries (i.e., Chile, Malaysia): https://pip.worldbank.org/poverty-calculator\n14 Less than half of Mexican households own a computer and about 70 percent have access to the internet. Source :\nhttps://www.hertie-school.org/en/news/detail/content/covid-19-widens-the-digital-divide-in-mexico.\n15 Cervantes, A. et al., “Estimating the economic impact of interpersonal violence in Mexico in 2021: projecting three hypothetical\nscenarios for 2030”, Pan American Journal of Public Health”, March 2023, https://doi.org/10.26633/RPSP.2023.39.\n16 For instance, the substantial increment in the minimum wage, accumulating more than 100 percent increment between 2019 and\n2023 and the outsourcing labor reform, limiting the outsourcing of workers and enhancing formalization of workers.\n17 Investment has been subdued for more than a decade. By 2022, gross fixed capital formation was similar to that of 2012.\n2\n\nrecession. Economic activity returned to its pre-pandemic level in 2022, as GDP grew by 5.7 percent in 2021\nand 3.9 percent in 2022. Growth momentum has continued in 2023, with GDP growing by 3.2 percent year-\non-year.\n10. Smaller firms were most impacted during the pandemic.18 Gross private investment, which had\nstagnated for more than a decade, fell by 4.4 percent and 17.3 percent in real terms in 2019 and 2020,\nrespectively. It rebounded by 9.7 percent in 2021 and by 7.7 percent in 2022. Micro, small, and medium\nenterprises (MSMEs), which in 2019 represented over 99 percent of firms (and about 70 percent of\nemployment and 50 percent of value added), were less resilient during the pandemic, mainly due to their\nlower use of technology to maintain operations.19 Between mid-2019 and mid-2021, Mexico experienced a\nnet loss of about 400,000 MSMEs (1.6 million closures versus 1.2 million new firms). The decline in private\ninvestment during the pandemic was partially offset by higher public infrastructure investment in flagship\nprojects. Recent policy changes in the United States, 20 Mexico’s largest trading partner, have created a boom\nin nearshoring opportunities driven by manufacturing and industrial real estate. 21 During the first three\nquarters of 2023, investment has increased 19.7 percent, surpassing pre-pandemic levels, which could\ncatalyze investment and economic growth, especially in the north and central parts of Mexico.22\n11. Human development outcomes are still suffering from the impact of the pandemic. Mexico\nexperienced one of the highest levels of pandemic-related extraordinary deaths in the world, and among the\nlongest school closures in the region, resulting in about two years of learning losses on average.23 Transfer\nprograms did not significantly offset the effects on household incomes. Between 2016 and 2022, household\nincome from social protection programs (including pensions, scholarships, and other public transfers)\nincreased by 27.3 percent.24 Sweeping reforms to social protection, labor, health, and education programs\nhad only been partially completed by 2020, which led to gaps in coverage, particularly in social protection\nand health.25 Policy shifts, institutional challenges, and turnover in government also aggravated the\npandemic’s impact on inequality of access, opportunity, and outcomes in human development.26\n12. As Mexico has exited the pandemic crisis, the urgency of addressing global challenges has\nincreased, particularly climate change adaptation and mitigation, energy access, and water security and\naccess. Mexico is the second-largest greenhouse gas (GHG) emitter in the region, and among the top ten\nglobal emitters of methane.27 The effects of climate change via both extreme and slow-onset events have\nbeen increasingly felt, with higher disaster-related costs and greater human impact, particularly on the\npoorest.28 Mexican agriculture, tourism, water infrastructure, and transportation have all been increasingly\nhit by climate effects. Current patterns of urbanization, agricultural competition for water resources,\ndeforestation, and the weight of fossil fuel in the energy matrix all make meeting Mexico’s Nationally\nDetermined Contributions (NDC) harder, underscoring the need to advance further on key global challenges.\nTo this end, SHCP has focused on boosting sustainable finance, such as the sustainable taxonomy.\n18 World Bank, “Las Empresas Mexicanas durante la Pandemia de COVID-19,” June 2021.\n19 World Bank, “Unmasking the Impact of COVID-19 on Businesses: Firm Level Evidence from across the World,” 2020.\n20 CHIPS Act and Inflation Reduction Act.\n21 Foreign direct investment (FDI) inflows due to nearshoring could double in the next decade and domestic private investment will\ncomplement the supply chains of exporting industries and enabling sectors.\n22 Oxford Economics, “Mexico Nearshoring,” June 2023.\n23 Mexico registered the highest avoidable mortality rate of all member countries of the OECD, with a rate of 665 per 100,000\ninhabitants.www.eleconomista.com.mx/politica/Mexico-con-la-tasa-de-mortalidad-mas-alta-de-la-OCDE-202311160007.html\n24 INEGI (2023) Comunicado de prensa Núm. 420/23, page 2.\n25 Social assistance coverage in 2020 was similar to 2018, but lower for the poorest quintile. While social assistance coverage in 2022\nsurpassed its 2018 levels, it remains lower than in 2018 for the poorest quintile.\n26 World Bank. “Mexico: Social Protection Public Expenditure and Institutional Review,” 2023.\n27 Global Methane Tracker 2022 published by the International Energy Agency.\n28 The total cost of disasters increased from an average of MXN$22,388 million per year in 2000-2010 to MXN$30,220 million per\nyear in 2011-2022. It also recorded proactive restructurings, but noted that changing priorities, budget constraints, institutional turnover, procurement delays, and limited implementation capacity affected continuity and results.", "question_ids": ["5B.7", "8B.6", "8B.8"], "polarity": "mixed", "period_fit": "Directly covers the FY2020-2023 implementation record relevant to the end of López Obrador's 2023 period.", "ruler_attribution": "The review explicitly records government-requested cancellations and changing priorities, but also identifies World Bank and institutional factors.", "limitations": ["The record concerns the World Bank portfolio, not all federal projects.", "Cancellation can reflect adaptation or failure; the document gives differing project-specific reasons."], "start_unit": 70, "end_unit": 75, "locator": "page 70 through page 75", "exact_excerpt": "Annex 5. Program Implementation\nProgram and Portfolio Performance\n1. World Bank lending to Mexico has increased. As of December 2023, the World Bank active\nportfolio includes US$1.68 billion from the International Bank for Reconstruction and Development\n(IBRD), US$2.4 billion committed portfolio from the International Finance Corporation (IFC), and US$536\nmillion from the Multilateral Investment Guarantee Agency (MIGA). Under the current CPF, the total\nIBRD lending approved sums up to US$4.3 billion (US$1.1 billion average per year) relative to US$560.0\nmillion in the previous CPF. The International Finance Corporation (IFC) also committed investments\nincreased in the first four years relative to the same period of the previous CPF, from US$1.62 billion\nduring FY14-17 to US$1.75 billion during FY20-23. Responding to global context and increased client\nappetite for countercyclical financing sources, DPF increased to US$3.9 billion compared to US$0.5 billion\nunder the previous CPF.228 Over FY20-FY23, four Development Policy Financing (DPF) operations were\nextended to the Mexican authorities, three directly supporting the COVID-19 crisis response and\nrecovery, including IBRD’s first Build Back Better DPF. For FY24, the pipeline for the remaining CPF\nconsists of only one DPF project. In addition, IBRD has an active Bank-executed trust fund portfolio\nbenefiting the country which comprises 34 trust funds for a total net commitment of US$71.1 million.\n2. IBRD portfolio has moved from an average of sixteen projects during the previous CPF to ten\nprojects during the current CPF, with a gradual reduction of investment project financing (IPF). IPF was\nreduced from 11 projects in 2020 to 7 in 2023. The active IBRD portfolio as of December 2023, comprises\nfive IBRD loans (US$1.6 billion), complemented by two Global Environment Facility (GEF) projects, and\none recipient executed trust-funded project. Since the CPF was discussed by the Board, seven IBRD\nprojects and one GEF stand-alone project have been approved. During this same period, ten projects\nclosed, four operations were partially cancelled, four were prepared but dropped at later stages of\npreparation, and two were not signed resulting in withdrawn loan offers (Table 5.2).\nTable 5.1. Restructurings have been a key component of portfolio management, including: (i) partial\ncancellations (e.g., US$100 million of the Energy Efficiency in Public Facilities Project and US$45 million of\nthe Modernization of Public Financial Management Systems in Mexico Project); (ii) extensions of closing\ndates (including in half of the total restructurings to date – eleven extensions from FY20-23); (iii)\nadjustment to the Results Framework as well as changes to components and costs, are some of the\nproactive actions taken to address implementation bottlenecks.\n66\n\nTable 5.4.
